# Data ledger --- Book 4 (University Year 2)

Book 4's own rows. Shared rows are in `sources/data_ledger.md`; the rules are stated
there. Owned by the Book 4 writer; other books may cite these rows read-only.

| usgs:glossary-ch6 | phreatic eruption: steam-driven explosion when ground or surface water is heated by magma, lava, hot rocks or new deposits (basaltic lava up to 1170 C), ejecting steam, water, ash, blocks and bombs; phreatomagmatic eruption: magma and water interact explosively, ejecting steam and pyroclastic fragments; Plinian: large explosive events with columns into the stratosphere (>11 km), named after Pliny's description of Vesuvius AD 79; lava fountains typically 10-100 m high, occasionally more than 500 m; pyroclastic flow: hot (typically >800 C) mixture of rock fragments, gas and ash travelling tens of m/s from a vent or collapsing flow front; pyroclastic surge: ground-hugging clouds at hurricane velocities and several hundred C; block and ash flow: ash and angular fragments; lahar: mixture of water and volcanic debris moving rapidly downstream, consistency from muddy water to wet cement, formed by melting of snow and ice by pyroclastic flows, intense rain on loose deposits, breakout of a dammed lake, debris avalanches; caldera: basin-shaped depression 2-50 km across formed by collapse when magma is withdrawn from a shallow reservoir; ignimbrite: rock formed by deposition and consolidation of pyroclastic density currents (welded tuff, ash-flow tuff); tephra: any fragment forcibly ejected that travels an airborne path; eruption column: ascending vertical part above the vent, spreading laterally higher up into plumes or umbrella clouds; dome: steep-sided mass of viscous lava, typically rhyolite or dacite; effusive eruption: dominated by outpouring of lava; explosive eruption: energetic eruption producing mainly ash, pumice and ballistic debris; isopach: line of equal deposit thickness | 1170; 10-100; 500; 800; 2-50; 11 | C; m; m; C; km; km | USGS-VHP-GLOSSARY | 2026-10-23 |

| vp21:series | magma series (alkaline, calc-alkaline, tholeiitic) a cornerstone of petrology since the early 20th century; AFM diagram: A = Na2O + K2O, F = FeOT, M = MgO, A + F + M = 1; differentiation produces a Fenner trend (F/M increases with A: tholeiitic) or a Bowen trend (F/M more constant: calc-alkaline); the difference relates to oxygen fugacity (Osborn 1959): in reduced magmas Fe is removed slowly by Mg-rich ferrous minerals (olivine, pyroxene), Fe/Mg increases; oxidising conditions promote ferric-iron-bearing magnetite, removing Fe more efficiently; tholeiitic rocks at mid-ocean ridges, calc-alkaline rocks at subduction zones where slab dehydration supplies oxidised species (Kelley and Cottrell 2009); the boundary was inferred by eye and several are in use (Kuno 1968; Irvine and Baragar 1971); felsic compositions of the two series hard to separate on the AFM diagram; data set of Rollinson and Pease (2021): 456 tholeiitic rocks from Iceland and 580 calc-alkaline rocks from the Cascade Mountains; the tholeiitic trend has a dog-leg, from ferrous to ferric (magnetite) iron sequestration | 456; 580 | samples | VERMEESCH-PEASE-2021 | 2026-10-23 |

| scmr:types | types of metamorphism: regional (large area, large-scale tectonic processes: ocean-floor spreading, crustal thickening in plate collision, basin subsidence) versus local (attributable to a localized cause: magmatic intrusion, faulting, meteorite impact); orogenic; burial (deeply buried beneath thick sedimentary or volcano-sedimentary piles, not typically associated with deformation or magmatism, usually without schistosity, very low to intermediate T); ocean-floor (steep geothermal gradients near spreading centres, hot aqueous fluids, incomplete recrystallisation); thermal, contact (heat flow from a magmatic body into its country rocks), hydrothermal; dislocation (fault and shear zones, grain-size reduction, mylonites, cataclasites); impact (melting and vaporisation); prograde (rising T) and retrograde (falling T) | - | - | SCMR-TYPES-GC | 2026-10-23 |

(1959) added the zeolite facies and a prehnite-pumpellyite zone, Miyashiro (1973) used ten; facies series (Miyashiro 1961): a sequence of facies developed under the same range of P/T ratios, radial sectors on a P-T diagram | 1915; 1959; 1961; 1973; 10 | yr; yr; yr; yr; - | SCMR-TYPES-GC | 2026-10-23 |

| scmr:migmatite | migmatite: composite silicate rock, heterogeneous at the meso- to megascopic scale, typically of dark parts with metamorphic features and light parts of plutonic appearance; anatexis: melting of a rock, whatever the degree; leucosome: the lightest parts; melanosome: the darkest parts, mostly dark minerals, between leucosomes or rimming mesosome relicts; mesosome: intermediate colour, a more or less modified relict of the parent rock; paleosome: the parent rock; neosome: the newly formed part; metatexite: discrete, well-defined leucosomes, mesosomes and melanosomes; diatexis: advanced anatexis in which dark minerals also melt, the melt not removed; diatexite: light and dark parts form schlieren and nebulitic structures that merge; nebulite: diffuse relicts of earlier rocks or structures; agmatite: brecciated structure | - | - | SCMR-MIGMATITES-GC | 2026-10-23 |
| bgs99:migmatite | migmatite (Mehnert 1968, adopted): a megascopically composite rock of two or more petrographically different parts, one the country rock in a more or less metamorphic stage, the other of pegmatitic, aplitic, granitic or generally plutonic appearance; leucosome leucocratic quartzofeldspathic, melanosome melanocratic complementary to it, mesosome like an ordinary schist or gneiss, neosome = leucosome + melanosome; structural qualifiers stromatic (layered), agmatitic (vein network, brecciated look), schlieric (streaks within leucosome), nebulitic (ghost-like relicts) | 1968 | year | BGS-RR-99-02 | 2026-10-23 |

| moyen17:trends | owing to the granitic minimum, every granitoid suite has leucocratic, mildly peraluminous samples, so peraluminous samples are no proof of crustal involvement; the discriminant is the shape of the whole trend: in Shand's diagram the Kohistan arc suite reaches A/NK about 1, A/CNK >= 1 along a steep negative array from high A/NK and low A/CNK, low-Ca S-type plutons along a positive array with A/NK about A/CNK; S-type granites are peraluminous, two-mica or cordierite-bearing | 1 | - | MOYEN-2017-LITHOS | 2026-10-23 |
| schmid04:alpine-tethys | the Alps record the closure of ocean basins during convergence of the African and European plates; the Alpine Tethys comprises the Piedmont-Liguria and Valais oceans, kinematically linked to the opening of the Atlantic; drifting of the Piedmont-Liguria ocean started in the Middle Jurassic (opening of the Central Atlantic); radiolarites and aptychus limestones followed the onset of spreading and are diagnostic of it; the Valais ocean, a narrow northern branch, probably began to open in earliest Cretaceous times and closed during the Middle to Late Eocene; the Briançonnais ribbon continent lay between the two branches and wedges out eastwards between the Engadine and Tauern windows | - | - | SCHMID-2004-ECLOGAE | 2026-10-23 |
| schmid04:apulia | "Apulian plate" denotes all continental realms south of the Alpine Tethys (Piedmont-Liguria ocean) and north of Neotethys; the Austroalpine nappes are remnants of the southern margin of the Piedmont-Liguria ocean (Apulian plate) north of the Periadriatic Line; the Margna-Sesia fragment (Sesia-Lanzo zone, Dent Blanche nappe) was rifted off the most distal Apulian margin during the mid-Jurassic opening; the Southern Alps are the part of the Apulian plate south of the Periadriatic Line, a dominantly south-verging, mostly Miocene fold-and-thrust belt (retro-wedge) 10 to 15 km thick | 10-15 | km | SCHMID-2004-ECLOGAE | 2026-10-23 |
| schmid04:units | Penninic nappes: slivers detached from subducted European margin, Valais ocean, Piedmont-Liguria ocean and the Briançonnais terrane, accreted as thin slices (nappes) to the Apulian upper plate; Upper Penninic (Piedmont-Liguria: ophiolites, relicts of oceanic lithosphere and/or exhumed subcontinental mantle, Bündnerschiefer/schistes lustrés) normally occupy the structurally highest Penninic position; Middle Penninic = Briançonnais; Lower Penninic = Valais (North-Penninic ophiolites and Bündnerschiefer); Subpenninic = European basement nappes (Lepontine dome, Tauern window); Helvetic and Ultrahelvetic nappes: limestone-dominated cover nappes of the proximal European margin, a thin-skinned fold-and-thrust belt detached from its basement, base drawn along the Glarus overthrust | - | - | SCHMID-2004-ECLOGAE | 2026-10-23 |
| schmid04:two-orogenies | the Alps are the product of two orogenies, a Cretaceous one (closure of an embayment of the Meliata ocean into Apulia; Eoalpine; high-pressure peak reached at around 100 Ma south of the Northern Calcareous Alps) followed by a Tertiary one (closure of the Alpine Tethys between Apulia and Europe); during the Cretaceous the southern (Apulian) margin of the Piedmont-Liguria basin was converted into an active margin (trench deposits: Bündnerschiefer, schistes lustrés, calcescisti) | 100 | Ma | SCHMID-2004-ECLOGAE | 2026-10-23 |

| schmid04:windows | around the Tauern window the Austroalpine nappes overlie, along a Tertiary thrust, Penninic units (slivers of the distal European margin and oceanic slivers of the Alpine Tethys); the central crystalline core of the Tauern window and its cover are attributed to the European margin; in the Engadine window Bündnerschiefer (Valais ocean) lie below the easternmost Briançonnais remnants (Tasna nappe), with blueschists preserved; only very small Austroalpine klippen (Northern Calcareous Alps units) are preserved in Central Switzerland; detached cover nappes form the Préalpes Romandes and the 'Klippen' of Central Switzerland at the front of the Western Alps | - | - | SCHMID-2004-ECLOGAE | 2026-10-23 |
| schmid04:foreland | the Oligocene-Miocene Molasse Basin is the northern flexural foreland basin of the Alps, well developed in Switzerland and Bavaria; it began to subside during the late Eocene; orogen-derived continental clastics were deposited during the late Oligocene to late Miocene, directly following accretionary-wedge formation preserved in Lower Oligocene flysch units on top of the Helvetic nappes, later thrust onto the Molasse Basin; the Subalpine Molasse is made of south-dipping thrust slices of conglomeratic Molasse; Late Neogene (Plio-Pleistocene) sediments of the Po Plain partly overlie Alpine structures unconformably; the Austrian Molasse narrows to less than 10 km near the Bohemian Massif spur | 10 | km | SCHMID-2004-ECLOGAE | 2026-10-23 |
| schmid04:skins | the external massifs and their cover (Chaînes Subalpines, para-autochthonous Helvetic units) were strongly affected by Neogene (Miocene) thick-skinned thrusting; thin-skinned thrust propagation in the Late Miocene to Pliocene led to folding and thrusting in the Jura; the basement of the external massifs is connected below the Molasse basin and the Jura; the Tavetsch slice is thrust over the Aar massif by at least 15 km | 15 | km | SCHMID-2004-ECLOGAE | 2026-10-23 |

| mckerrow00:caledonian | Caledonian Orogeny redefined as all the Cambrian, Ordovician, Silurian and Devonian tectonic events of the development and closure of the parts of the Iapetus Ocean between Laurentia (to the NW) and Baltica and Avalonia (to the SE and E); a series of orogenic phases (arc-arc, arc-continent and continent-continent collisions); the whole orogeny occupied about 200 Ma; deformation events c. 540-400 Ma have all been called Caledonian; the margins rifted c. 600 Ma; the term should not be used to indicate an age | 200; 540-400; 600 | Myr; Ma; Ma | MCKERROW-2000-JGS | 2026-10-23 |
| mckerrow00:phases | Penobscotian phase: obduction of the Penobscot arc onto the Ganderian/Avalonian margin in the Tremadoc, 490-485 Ma; Grampian (Scotland, Llanvirn) and Taconian/Humberian (Northern Appalachians) phases: arc-continent collisions with Laurentia from basal Arenig (480 Ma) to Caradoc (455 Ma); Shelveian phase (early Ashgill): Avalonia-Baltica collision and closure of the Tornquist Sea; Scandian phase (Wenlock-Emsian): emplacement of the major nappes in Scandinavia; Acadian phase (Emsian) in the Northern Appalachians and much of the southern British Isles; earlier phases arc-arc or arc-continent, later ones (Shelveian, Scandian, Acadian) continent-continent; phases often diachronous; map of the Iapetus Ocean in the Caradoc, c. 455 Ma | 490-485; 480; 455 | Ma; Ma; Ma | MCKERROW-2000-JGS | 2026-10-23 |
| schiffer20:wilson | Wilson's question of 1966, "Did the Atlantic close and then re-open?", gave rise to the Wilson Cycle concept: closure and reopening of oceans along former orogens, the weakest zones of a disintegrating continent; it works well in the Central Atlantic, where the new ocean closely tracks the parallel Appalachians; breakup between Scandinavia and Greenland generally follows the Caledonian orogen, but farther south the Iapetus suture is preserved and runs through northern England and central Ireland, leaving pieces of Laurentian cratonic crust on Europe's north-western seaboard (Rockall-Hatton margin); the Labrador Sea and Baffin Bay cut through cratons almost orthogonally across Precambrian orogenic belts; the Wilson Cycle is at best an imperfect model for the region | 1966 | year | SCHIFFER-2020-ESR | 2026-10-23 |
| schiffer20:reactivation | structural inheritance: zones of pre-existing weakness preserved in the continental lithosphere can be rejuvenated many times; rejuvenation includes reactivation, the repeated focussing of deformation along discrete pre-existing structures (faults, shear zones, lithological contacts), and reworking (repeated metamorphism, ductile deformation, magmatism in the same lithospheric volume); long thermal relaxation (more than 30-50 Myr) can produce a cold, strong lithosphere that impedes reactivation | 30-50 | Myr | SCHIFFER-2020-ESR | 2026-10-23 |
| schiffer20:caledonides | Laurussia formed by closure of the Iapetus Ocean and Tornquist seaway and collision of Laurentia, Baltica and Avalonia, culminating in the Scandian phase at 425-400 Ma, preceded by arc accretion (Finnmarkian, Grampian, Taconian) in the late Cambrian-early Ordovician; Avalonia the first of several large terranes released from Gondwana, opening the Rheic Ocean in the mid-Ordovician; peri-Gondwana terranes Armorica and Megumia docked south of Avalonia in the Early Devonian (Acadian); Caledonian metamorphism and intrusions in East Greenland and Scandinavia 500-360 Ma, main Scandian phase about 425 Ma | 425-400; 500-360; 425 | Ma; Ma; Ma | SCHIFFER-2020-ESR | 2026-10-23 |
| schiffer20:variscan | Variscan-Appalachian orogeny: continent-continent collision with Gondwana and peri-Gondwanan terranes and microcontinents, released episodically (probably by back-arc spreading on the Gondwana margin) and docked successively against Laurasia; it ended with collision between Gondwana and Laurasia in Late Carboniferous-Permian time, forming a fold belt running E-W through southern Europe and NE-SW between eastern North America and NW Africa; Europe, North America and Greenland were part of Laurasia, the northern constituent of Pangaea, before the North Atlantic opened | - | - | SCHIFFER-2020-ESR | 2026-10-23 |
| chenin18:cycle | the Wilson cycle (term of Dewey and Burke 1974) is the rift-drift-subduction-collision-collapse evolution occurring repeatedly along particular deformation belts; it originates from Wilson (1966), who recognised from the palaeofaunas of North America and Western Europe a major ocean in the lower and middle Palaeozoic that no longer existed from the late Palaeozoic to the middle Mesozoic; many orogens result from closing embryonic or immature rift systems (Alps, Pyrenees, most of the Variscides of Western Europe, narrow, under 500 km), others from wide mature oceans (Scandinavian Caledonides); orogenic collapse: gravitational re-equilibration of an over-thickened lithosphere after orogeny, by normal and low-angle detachment faults in the high domain, thrusting towards the lower surroundings and strike-slip faults; magma-poor (Caledonian) or magma-rich (Variscan; flat Moho, layered lower crust); thermal heterogeneities re-equilibrate in a few tens of Myr | 1974; 1966; 500 | year; year; km | CHENIN-2018-GSLSP | 2026-10-23 |
| faure24:variscan | Variscan orogeny polycyclic and multi-collisional over about 260 Myr, from the Ediacaran (about 550 Ma) to the Late Carboniferous (290 Ma); the Western Alps a single cycle from the Late Triassic (about 225 Ma) to the Miocene (about 10 Ma); the Variscan segment from SW England and Belgium to S France: northern foreland in Laurussia, Rhenohercynian, Saxothuringian, Armorica microcontinent, Moldanubian, southern foreland in Gondwana, separated by ophiolitic sutures (Rheic, Tepla-Le Conquet, Eo-Variscan); metamorphic gradients about 8 C/km (Alps) and 30 C/km (Variscan); crustal melting (migmatites, granites) the major difference from the Western Alps; Carboniferous migmatites 330-325 Ma; late- to post-orogenic unthickening by extension, retrogression and intramontane basins; a Permian-Triassic peneplain developed on the eroded Variscan chain | 260; 550; 290; 8; 30; 330-325 | Myr; Ma; Ma; C/km; C/km; Ma | FAURE-2024-CRG | 2026-10-23 |
| nance22:supercontinents | supercontinents at c. 620-580 Ma (Pannotia, its existence debated), c. 950-800 Ma (Rodinia), c. 1.6-1.4 Ga (Nuna or Columbia), possible supercontinents or supercratons at c. 2.7-2.5 Ga (Kenorland) and c. 3.0 Ga (Ur), and Pangea c. 325-200 Ma; supercontinents assembled and broke up at intervals of roughly half a billion years, perhaps since the late Archean; the cycle is linked to global orogenesis, granitoid magmatism and zircon age peaks, crustal growth, mineralization, large igneous provinces, mantle convection, sea level and climate (amalgamation tends to coincide with cooling) | 620-580; 950-800; 1600-1400; 2700-2500; 3000; 325-200; 500 | Ma; Ma; Ma; Ma; Ma; Ma; Myr | NANCE-2022-NYAS | 2026-10-23 |

| cs14:subsidence | two overlapping processes: (1) loading by the growing volcano, a rapid phase lasting perhaps 1 Myr that submerges shorelines and reefs by more than 1 km, e.g. 2.6 m/kyr off the northwest coast of Hawaii from dated drowned reefs (Ludwig et al. 1991); (2) thermal contraction of the ageing lithosphere, ~0.01 m/kyr along much of the Hawaiian Ridge and Emperor Seamounts; a volcano submerged by more than the glacioeustatic variations (~125 m) is unlikely to reemerge; reefs drown during deglacial sea-level rise | 1; 1; 2.6; 0.01; 125 | Myr; km; m/kyr; m/kyr; m | USGS-PP1801-3-PDF | 2026-10-23 |
| cs14:arch | uplift on the Hawaiian Arch: on Oahu coral reef deposits of substage 5e (133-115 ka) exposed around the island; a ~334-ka coral reef stands 21 m above sea level | 133-115; 334; 21 | ka; ka; m | USGS-PP1801-3-PDF | 2026-10-23 |
| cs14:erase | subsidence coupled with erosion erases any volcanic remnant above sea level in about 15 Myr; Gardner Pinnacles, of that age, the oldest surviving island with subaerial volcanic rock; beyond it small sand islands and atolls; at least six reefs northwest of Hawaii form a stairstep configuration, the oldest deepest | 15; 6 | Myr; reefs | USGS-PP1801-3-PDF | 2026-10-23 |
| cs14:darwin-point | reefs of the Hawaiian chain drown about 33 Myr after the underlying volcanoes formed, when the last atolls submerge to become guyots (Grigg's Darwin Point); all volcanoes west of Midway and Kure are submerged, most are guyots; at Koko Seamount carbonate deposition lasted from about 50 to 16 Ma until deep-water coralline algae submerged below their growth limit of -150 m; subsidence, northward movement into cooler waters and rapid climate change are all required, because corals can grow faster than thermal subsidence | 33; 50; 16; 150 | Myr; Ma; Ma; m | USGS-PP1801-3-PDF | 2026-10-23 |

| odp200:nuuanu | the Nuuanu Landslide from the northeast flank of Koolau Volcano (Oahu) is the largest Hawaiian landslide, a debris avalanche extending more than 200 km from the island, spread over 23,000 km2, with blocks such as Tuscaloosa Seamount about 30 km long, 17 km wide and at least 2 km tall; it crossed the deep moat and travelled over 100 km uphill onto the Hawaiian Arch; age estimated at 2.1-1.8 Ma; turbidites at Site 1223 dated 1.77-1.95 Ma; Koolau surface flows 1.8-2.6 Ma | 200; 23000; 30; 17; 2; 100; 2.1-1.8 | km; km2; km; km; km; km; Ma | ODP-200-IR-NUUANU | 2026-10-23 |

| hoffman17:hysteresis | Fig. 1: ice-line latitude against solar or CO2 forcing in a 1D energy-balance model of the Budyko-Sellers type has stable branches and an unstable regime; with lower forcing the ice line reaches the ice-albedo instability threshold and advances uncontrollably to the equator; volcanic outgassing raises CO2 over millions to tens of millions of years to the deglaciation threshold; then the ice-albedo feedback drives the ice line poleward in about 2 kyr; silicate weathering and carbon burial lower CO2 in about 10^7 years; several stable climates are possible with present-day forcing | 2; 1e7 | kyr; yr | HOFFMAN-2017-PMC | 2026-10-23 |

| hoffman17:history | for 50 years climate models of the Budyko-Sellers type (Budyko 1968, 1969) have hinted that the Earth is vulnerable to global glaciation through ice-albedo feedback; the recognition that a panglacial state might be self-terminating (Walker, Hays and Kasting 1981) meant that it could not be ruled out on grounds of irreversibility; the term Snowball Earth and the hypothesis from Kirschvink (1992); Harland (1964) on evidence of late Precambrian glaciation; the term cryochron proposed by Hoffman and others (1998, A Neoproterozoic Snowball Earth); before 2004 no Cryogenian glacial deposit had been directly dated radiometrically | 1964; 1968; 1969; 1981; 1992; 1998; 2004 | year | HOFFMAN-2017-PMC | 2026-10-23 |

| th23:pitman | the globally averaged rate of oceanic spreading is often taken as the dominant control on long-term global sea level: longer total ridge length or faster ridges make more young, warm, buoyant lithosphere, raising the ocean basins and displacing sea water onto the continents (the Pitman model); mass must be conserved, so uplift of the ocean floor must be balanced by subsidence elsewhere; hypsometric fulcrum at the mean depth of the ridges, 2.6 km (-2600 m) | 2.6 | km | TASISTRO-HART-2023-PMC | 2026-10-23 |
| th23:flooding | Marine sediments across North America record several Phanerozoic flooding events, beginning with the Sauk transgression above the Great Unconformity; the Sloss sequences track six to seven continent-scale transgressions; flooding of North America matches in space and time the subduction facing the continent (dynamic subsidence), except the Cambrian Sauk; the aggregated signal is an exaggerated global M-curve of Phanerozoic sea level; flooded area decreases from the Carboniferous to the end Triassic as Pangaea forms, Permian-Triassic sea-level low; flooding increases again in the Jurassic and Cretaceous (Zuni I and II); the Tippecanoe sequence extrapolated globally suggests 100-200 m more sea level in the Late Ordovician; subduction drives subsidence over hundreds of km, while continents are thousands of km across | 6-7; 100-200 | sequences; m | TASISTRO-HART-2023-PMC | 2026-10-23 |

| marshall23:big-five | Raup and Sepkoski (1982) identified five episodes of elevated extinction in the marine record (the Big Five): end-Ordovician, Late Devonian, end-Permian, end-Triassic, end-Cretaceous, from family extinctions per Myr in 76 stages; the Big Five are not statistically distinct from background extinction, intervals in the Cambrian and early Ordovician had even higher rates; they remain the five largest since the early Ordovician; the end-Permian genus-level intensity is 56 % (Bambach 2006) and its species-level loss about 81 % (Stanley 2016), not the often quoted 96 %; some events were sudden, others lasted hundreds of thousands of years or longer | 76; 56; 81; 96 | stages; %; %; % | MARSHALL-2023-PMC | 2026-10-23 |
| marshall23:definition | Sepkoski (1986): a mass extinction is any substantial increase in the amount of extinction suffered by more than one geographically widespread higher taxon during a relatively short interval of geologic time, resulting in an at least temporary decline in their standing diversity | - | - | MARSHALL-2023-PMC | 2026-10-23 |
| kaiho22:table | major mass extinctions at 444, 372, 252, 201 and 66 Ma, driven by large volcanic eruptions and projectile impacts; Table 1 marine genus extinction (%), Sepkoski 1996 / Bambach 2006 / Stanley 2016 columns: K-Pg (66 Ma) 45 / 40 / 39; end-Triassic (201.4) 46 / 43 / -; end-Permian (251.9) 66 / 56 / 62; Frasnian-Famennian (372) 41 / 35 / 18; end-Ordovician (445-444) 52 / 40 / 45.5; end-Guadalupian (259.8) 48 / 48 / 34; marine species losses 80-86 % end-Permian, 70-73 % end-Triassic, 68-72 % K-Pg; the five range 18-62 % (genera) and 35-83 % (species) | 444; 372; 252; 201; 66 | Ma | KAIHO-2022-BG | 2026-10-23 |
| stanley16:permian | only about 81 % of marine species died out in the terminal Permian crisis, not the 90-96 % often quoted (which combined the Middle and Late Permian extinctions); about 90 orders and more than 220 families of marine animals survived | 81; 90; 220 | %; orders; families | STANLEY-2016-PMC | 2026-10-23 |
| rigo24:oaes | Phanerozoic oceanic anoxic events (OAEs) are intervals of widespread oxygen depletion in the ocean, recognised by sedimentological and/or geochemical records; conventionally black and dark laminated shales without bioturbation, rich in organic matter, with pyrite; often with carbon-isotope excursions and warming (low d18O); well studied: early Toarcian (T-OAE, about 183 Ma), early Aptian (OAE 1a, about 120 Ma), early Albian (OAE 1b, about 111 Ma), Cenomanian-Turonian (Bonarelli event, OAE 2, about 94 Ma); OAE 1a correlates with the Ontong Java Plateau, OAE 2 with the Caribbean-Colombian Plateau and the T-OAE with the Karoo-Ferrar flood basalts | 183; 120; 111; 94 | Ma | RIGO-2024-PMC | 2026-10-23 |
| wt24:oae2 | OAE 2 at the Cenomanian/Turonian boundary, about 94 Ma, in the super-hot-house mid-Cretaceous with high CO2 and exceptionally high sea level; one of several Mesozoic periods of widespread ocean anoxia, with organic-rich shales, marine extinctions and vegetation changes; positive carbon-isotope excursion about 5 permil in organic matter and 2-3 permil in carbonate; mercury spikes point to Kerguelen volcanism as the trigger | 94; 5; 2-3 | Ma; permil; permil | WALKER-TRIVETT-2024-PMC | 2026-10-23 |

| nws4:cape | CAPE: a measure of the energy available for convection, directly related to the maximum potential vertical speed in an updraft; observed values in thunderstorm environments often exceed 1000 J/kg, in extreme cases 5000 J/kg; no threshold above which severe weather is imminent; the area between the environmental temperature and the path of a rising parcel where the parcel is warmer (positive area) | 1000; 5000 | J/kg | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |

| nws4:lfc-el | level of free convection: the level at which a parcel of saturated air becomes warmer than the surrounding air and begins to rise freely, most readily in a conditionally unstable atmosphere; equilibrium level: above the LFC, where the parcel temperature again equals the environment's, the height at which updrafts no longer accelerate, close to the height of thunderstorm tops | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |

| nws4:front | front: a boundary or transition zone between two air masses of different density, and thus usually of different temperature, named after the advancing air mass; frontogenesis: the formation of a front or the increase of the horizontal density gradient; katafront: a front where the warm air descends the frontal surface; frontal inversion: a temperature inversion aloft where warm air overruns the cold air behind a front | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:occluded | occluded front: a composite of two fronts, formed as a cold front overtakes a warm or quasi-stationary front; cold occlusion when the coldest air is behind the cold front (it slides under the warm front, lifting it aloft), warm occlusion when the coldest air is ahead of the warm front (the cold front rises over it) | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:stationary | stationary (quasi-stationary) front: a front between warm and cold air masses that is moving very slowly or not at all | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:extratropical | extratropical cyclone: a cyclone in the middle and high latitudes often 2000 km in diameter, usually containing a cold front that extends toward the equator for hundreds of kilometres; cyclogenesis: the formation or intensification of a cyclone or low-pressure system; warm sector: the region of warm surface air between a cold front and a warm front; comma cloud: a synoptic-scale comma-shaped cloud pattern on satellite images of large intense lows; dry slot: a zone of dry, relatively cloud-free air wrapping into the southern and eastern parts of a low; baroclinic zone: a region with a temperature gradient on a constant-pressure surface, favoured for strengthening and weakening systems, with wind shear | 2000 | km | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:polar-front | polar front: a semipermanent, semicontinuous front separating tropical air masses from polar air masses; polar jet: marked by a concentration of isotherms and strong vertical shear, the boundary between polar and subtropical air, the high-speed core of the prevailing westerlies, associated with the location and motion of mid-latitude highs and lows, farther south and stronger in winter | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:rossby | Rossby waves: a series of troughs and ridges on quasi-horizontal surfaces in the major belt of upper-tropospheric westerlies, thousands of kilometres long with significant latitudinal amplitude; trough: an elongated area of relatively low pressure, usually without a closed circulation, the opposite of ridge; ridge: an elongated area of relatively high pressure; longwave trough: large length and usually long duration; shortwave trough: a disturbance in the mid or upper troposphere which induces upward motion ahead of it; negative-tilt trough (axis south-east to north-west) often a sign of a developing or intensifying system; closed low aloft: partly or completely detached from the westerlies, moves slowly | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:jet-streak | jet streak (jet max): a point or area of relative maximum wind speeds within a jet stream | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:nwp | numerical weather prediction: a computer forecast based on equations governing the motions and forces affecting motion of fluids, initialized on specified conditions at a certain place and time; ensemble forecast: multiple predictions from slightly different initial conditions and/or versions of models, to improve accuracy by averaging and to give information on forecast uncertainty | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| ucar4:stationary-occluded | a stationary front forms when a cold or warm front stops moving, neither air mass strong enough to move the other; winds parallel to the front help it stay; it may stay for days, then move again as a cold or warm front or break apart; often cloudy with rain or snow; drawn with alternating red semicircles and blue triangles pointing to opposite sides; an occluded front shares characteristics of warm and cold fronts, drawn purple with semicircles and triangles, formed when a cold front follows right behind a warm front | - | - | UCAR-FRONTS-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| aoml:tc-genesis | tropical cyclogenesis needs: warm ocean water of at least 26.5 degC (80 F) through a sufficient depth, at least of order 50 m (150 ft); an atmosphere cooling fast enough with height to be potentially unstable to moist convection; relatively moist layers near 5 km; at least about 500 km (300 mi) from the equator (Coriolis force for near gradient-wind balance); a pre-existing near-surface disturbance with vorticity and convergence; vertical wind shear below about 10 m/s between the surface and the upper troposphere; necessary but not sufficient | 26.5 | degC | AOML-TCFAQ-A15 | 2026-10-23 |
| gilford21:pi | potential intensity (Bister and Emanuel 1998, Emanuel 2003): Vmax^2 = (Ck/CD) ((Ts - T0)/T0) (h*o - h*), Ck and CD the enthalpy and momentum surface exchange coefficients, h*o the saturation moist static energy at the sea surface, h* that of the air above the boundary layer; Ts sea-surface temperature, T0 outflow temperature; Ts/T0 represents dissipative heating; Carnot cycle: isothermal inflow gaining entropy at Ts, adiabatic ascent in the eyewall, isothermal radiative loss in the outflow at T0, adiabatic descent; Ck/CD = 0.9 typical default; annual-mean tropical outflow temperatures around 200 K, outflow levels below 100 hPa; observed storms rarely attain their PI | 0.9 | - | GILFORD-2021 | 2026-10-23 |
| wk24:scale | Saffir--Simpson category 5: 70 m/s or greater; of 197 category-5 tropical cyclones in 1980--2021, half occurred in the last 17 years; five recent storms exceeded 86 m/s (the proposed category 6); potential intensity: Emanuel's Carnot-engine model, v^2 ~ Ts - T0 | 70; 86 | m/s | WEHNER-KOSSIN-2024 | 2026-10-23 |
| krish19:predictability | Lorenz (1963) showed chaos in a three-variable model (sensitive dependence on initial conditions); first numerical forecast on ENIAC by Charney and others in 1950; GARP planning group estimated error doubling time about 5 days; Lorenz (1982), from ECMWF operational forecasts, doubling time of small errors 2.4 days; small errors grow first, then slow by nonlinearity to saturation; reliable predictability of the instantaneous weather less than about 10 days | 2.4; 5; 10 | days | KRISHNAMURTHY-2019 | 2026-10-23 |

| rr19:conveyor | conceptual airflows in extratropical cyclones (Carlson 1980, Browning 1997): warm conveyor belt, cold conveyor belt and dry intrusion; dry intrusions are streams of upper-tropospheric or lower-stratospheric air descending towards the surface with low wet-bulb potential temperature, typically behind a cold front, fanning out near the surface | - | - | RAVEH-RUBIN-2019 | 2026-10-23 |
| wang26:blocking | atmospheric blocking: a large, persistent high-pressure system, sometimes with a low-pressure region, that disrupts the normal eastward flow of the jet stream, causing extremes in mid- to high latitudes; blocks are quasi-stationary, persistent anticyclonic anomalies; almost all detection methods require a persistence of at least 5 days; late June to early July 2021 a strong block led to the Pacific Northwest heatwave, over 1000 deaths | 5 | days | WANG-2026-BLOCKING | 2026-10-23 |
| aoml:tc-weaken | a tropical cyclone passing through an area of high vertical wind shear or dry air could be weakened; too much wind shear disrupts or weakens the convection; dry mid-level air ingested into thunderstorms hinders development | - | - | AOML-TCFAQ-A15 | 2026-10-23 |

| usgs10b:share | porphyry copper deposits: the world's largest source (about 60 percent) and resource (about 65 percent) of copper; average grade 0.44 percent Cu in 2008 (0.49 percent in 2002); large size, commonly hundreds of millions to billions of metric tons; grades from several tenths of 1 percent to more than 1 weight percent; more than 98 percent of the ore is gangue | 60; 65; 0.44; 98 | %; %; %; % | USGS-SIR-2010-5070B | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10b:form | porphyry copper deposits centred on small cylindrical porphyry stocks or dike swarms (plan areas of ore-related intrusions 0.2-0.5 km2); deposits typically 0.1 to 1.0 km in diameter with similar vertical dimensions; form in the upper crust at less than 5-10 km depth at convergent margins, prone to erosion, so more than 90 percent of known deposits are Cenozoic or Mesozoic; vertical extent of hypogene copper ore generally 1 to 1.5 km or less; supergene enriched ore seldom more than 200 m; altered rock may extend 10 km or farther from the centre; many deposits related to intermediate to felsic calc-alkaline magmas in arcs above subduction zones | 0.1-1.0; 5-10; 90; 1-1.5; 200 | km; km; %; km; m | USGS-SIR-2010-5070B | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10b:alteration | potassic alteration (K-feldspar or biotite, with bornite, chalcopyrite, pyrite, magnetite) is central, deeper, higher temperature and earlier; sericitic alteration (sericite, quartz, pyrite) lies upward and outward, along D veins, pervasive in volumes up to 1 km3; advanced argillic alteration generally overlies potassic and sericitic zones; propylitic alteration forms at shallow to moderate depths peripheral to the central zones; sodic-calcic alteration at deep levels; quartz veins precipitated at about 700 to 500 C; retrograde quartz solubility at 350 to 500 C; epithermal quartz-alunite environment overlies some porphyry systems | 700-500; 350-500 | C; C | USGS-SIR-2010-5070B | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10b:fluids | magmatic fluids from more than 5 km depth (more than 1.4 kbar) form a single supercritical phase of about 3-5 weight percent salinity, which separates into high-salinity brine and lower-salinity vapour on decompression; both carry metals; early magmatic fluids contain elevated Cu, Fe, Zn, Pb and Mn; volatile separation (second boiling) causes hydrofracturing | 5; 1.4; 3-5 | km; kbar; wt % | USGS-SIR-2010-5070B | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10b:supergene | supergene ore precipitated from descending, low-pH groundwater that dissolved hypogene copper minerals and redeposited copper; the overlying porous rock from which copper was removed is the leached capping, 0 to several hundred metres thick; enriched copper sulfide zones mostly tens to several hundred metres thick (up to 750 m), tens of megatons to more than 1.5 gigatons at 0.4 to 1.7 percent Cu, invariably higher grade than the underlying hypogene ore; oxide zones tens to several hundred megatons at 0.3 (0.34) to more than 1 percent Cu; leached-capping iron oxides (goethite, hematite, jarosite) used to interpret the precursor sulfides | 0.4-1.7; 1.5; 750 | % Cu; Gt; m | USGS-SIR-2010-5070B | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10b:cutoffs | Batu Hijau (Indonesia): 914 Mt at 0.53 percent Cu and 0.40 g/t Au at a 0.3 percent Cu cutoff grade (Clode and others 1999), against 1,640 Mt at 0.44 percent Cu and 0.45 g/t Au in Singer and others (2008); El Teniente (Chile): 20,031 Mt at 0.56 percent Cu at a 0.2 percent cutoff (CODELCO 2007), 20,731 Mt at 0.62 percent in Singer and others (2008), the world's largest known Cu-Mo deposit | 914; 0.53; 0.40; 1640; 0.44; 20031; 0.56; 0.2 | Mt; %; g/t; Mt; %; Mt; %; % | USGS-SIR-2010-5070B | 2026-10-23 |

| usgs10c:vms | volcanogenic massive sulfide deposits form at or near the seafloor; sizes from pods of less than a ton to Rio Tinto (Spain) 1.5 Bt, Kholodrina 300 Mt, Windy Craggy 300 Mt, Brunswick No. 12 230 Mt, Ducktown 163 Mt; ages from 3.55 Ga to zero-age deposits forming at mid-ocean ridges, island arcs and back-arc basins; massive ore more than 40 percent sulfides (pyrite, pyrrhotite, chalcopyrite, sphalerite, galena); stringer or feeder (stockwork) zones beneath; metal zonation from Fe+Cu at the base to Zn+Fe+-Pb+-Ba at the top and margins; high-temperature (350 C) black smoker vents first discovered on the East Pacific Rise in 1979; iron staining and gossans at the surface helped early discovery | 1.5; 300; 230; 163; 3.55; 40; 350; 1979 | Bt; Mt; Mt; Mt; Ga; %; C; year | USGS-SIR-2010-5070C | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10i:r-factor | Campbell and Naldrett (1979): in a closed system the concentration of a metal in a sulfide liquid Cs = CL D (R + 1)/(R + D), CL the concentration in the initial silicate liquid, D the sulfide/silicate liquid partition coefficient, R the mass ratio of silicate magma to sulfide that reached equilibrium together; Cu and Ni D = 10^2 to 10^3, PGE D = 10^3 to 10^4; R of 100 to 2,000 gives sulfides with typical Ni-Cu ore contents but low Pt and Pd; R of 10,000 to 100,000 gives much higher Pt and Pd, as at Noril'sk and in reefs such as the Merensky Reef (Bushveld); parental magmas from at least 20-25 percent mantle melting, MgO more than 12 wt %; by 11 percent melting all sulfide of the source has dissolved | 100-2000; 10000-100000; 20-25; 12; 11 | -; -; %; wt %; % | USGS-SIR-2010-5070I | 2026-10-23 |

| usgs10a:mvt | MVT deposits: epigenetic, not associated with igneous activity, hosted mainly by dolostone and limestone in platform carbonate sequences at basin flanks or foreland thrust belts; sphalerite, galena, pyrite, marcasite, dolomite, calcite; basinal brines with about 10-30 wt % salts; deposition typically 75 C to about 200 C; replacement of carbonate and open-space fill; about 24 percent of the global Pb and Zn resources of sediment- and volcanic-hosted deposits and 38 percent of the sediment-hosted Pb and Zn tonnage; on average a Zn:Pb mass ratio of about 10:1 | 10-30; 75-200; 24; 38; 10 | wt %; C; %; %; - | USGS-SIR-2010-5070A | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10n:sedex | sedex deposits account for more than 50 percent of the world's Zn and Pb reserves and more than 25 percent of current production; more than 129 deposits in 25 basins; Mt Isa-McArthur basins 7 deposits with 112 Mt Zn+Pb metal; laminated or bedded sulfides in carbonaceous shales and siltstones of intracratonic rift basins; sphalerite and galena precipitated on or just below the sea floor from brines of about 100-200 C and 17-30 percent total dissolved solids rising along synsedimentary faults, mixing with locally produced H2S; metals transported dominantly as chloride complexes, so salinity is a primary control on solubility; at the Tom deposit a decrease of the cutoff grade by about 1 percent Zn+Pb would multiply the reserves by 5 | 50; 25; 129; 112; 100-200; 17-30; 5 | %; %; deposits; Mt; C; %; - | USGS-SIR-2010-5070N | 2026-10-23 |

| usgs10l:heavy | heavy minerals: specific gravity greater than 2.85 (quartz about 2.65); ilmenite 4.7, zircon 4.7, rutile 4.2-4.3, magnetite 5.2; gold specific gravity 15-19.3, so gold is commonly deposited inland in fluvial placers before reaching the coast; grade of a heavy-mineral sands deposit usually the average heavy-mineral content in weight percent (example cut-off 5 percent heavy minerals) | 2.85; 2.65; 4.7; 15-19.3 | - | USGS-SIR-2010-5070L | 2026-10-23 |
| drew03:orogenic | low-sulfide quartz gold deposits (also called mesothermal, Mother Lode type, quartz-carbonate lode gold, synorogenic gold; Groves and others 1998 proposed "orogenic"): gold-only deposits in granite-greenstone terranes along major faults; near-neutral, CO2-rich hydrothermal fluid produced by metamorphic reactions, low salinity, deposition at about 350 C and 4 to 12 km depth (Bohlke 1999); gold transported as the reduced sulfur complex AuHS(H2S)3; great vertical continuity, e.g. Kolar (India) 3.2 km | 350; 4-12; 3.2 | C; km; km | USGS-OFR-03-077 | 2026-10-23 |
| grabreck22:kimberlite | kimberlites are the primary source of economic-grade diamonds and erupt preferentially near or through thick, ancient continental lithosphere; the majority of diamonds formed at the base of thick continental lithosphere; kimberlites originate from depths in excess of 120-150 km, magma ascending at speeds up to 20 m/s; lithosphere thicker than 150 km is a proposed proxy for cratons | 120-150; 20; 150 | km; m/s; km | GRABRECK-2022 | 2026-10-23 |

| usgs10q:alteration | epithermal deposits: advanced argillic alteration with kaolinite/dickite and (or) pyrophyllite produced by very low pH fluids; potassic alteration with quartz, adularia and (or) carbonate and illite, from near-neutral pH fluids, forms the core of low- and intermediate-sulfidation deposits; rapid water-table lowering, lateral groundwater flow, fluid boiling and mixing may be important factors in deposit formation | - | - | USGS-SIR-2010-5070Q | 2026-10-23 |
| usgs10b:remote | in areas of good exposure, airborne and spaceborne multispectral and hyperspectral imaging can map the hydrothermal minerals of sericitic, argillic, advanced argillic and propylitic alteration, iron oxides and silica of porphyry systems by their absorption features from the visible near-infrared to the thermal infrared | - | - | USGS-SIR-2010-5070B | 2026-10-23 |
| cpc:enso-faq | El Nino: warming of the central and east-central equatorial Pacific (date line to 120W), the warm phase of ENSO; originally an annual warming off the west coast of tropical South America; CPC declares onset when the 3-month mean SST departure exceeds 0.5 degC in 5N-5S, 170W-120W; La Nina the cool phase; ENSO: coherent year-to-year variations of SST, convective rainfall, surface pressure and circulation across the equatorial Pacific; ENSO-neutral near average; changes in tropical rainfall from Indonesia to South America, about half way around the world | 0.5 | degC | CPC-ENSOFAQ | 2026-10-23 |
| cpc:enso-faq-so | Southern Oscillation: fluctuations of air pressure between the western and eastern tropical Pacific; during El Nino higher than average pressure over Indonesia and the western tropical Pacific, lower over the eastern; SOI from the departures of the Tahiti minus Darwin pressure difference; large negative in El Nino, positive in La Nina | - | - | CPC-ENSOFAQ | 2026-10-23 |
| cpc:enso-faq-features | typical El Nino: SST 1.5-2.5 degC above average in the east-central equatorial Pacific, subsurface 3-6 degC above at thermocline depth; thermocline deeper than average, 150-175 m; weaker easterly trades, low-level westerlies in the west; enhanced rain in the east, suppressed over Indonesia and northern Australia; weaker Walker circulation; La Nina: SST 1-2 degC below, subsurface 2-4 below, thermocline 50-100 m, stronger easterlies, enhanced Walker circulation | 1.5-2.5; 150-175; 50-100 | degC; m; m | CPC-ENSOFAQ | 2026-10-23 |
| cpc:enso-faq-timing | El Nino and La Nina typically occur every 3-5 years, interval 2 to 7 years in the record; El Nino lasts typically 9-12 months, La Nina 1-3 years; develop March-June, peak December-April, weaken May-July; strongest in December-April because equatorial SSTs are warmest then; SST and rainfall anomalies change the winds, which amplify the SST anomalies: coupling of ocean and atmosphere | 3-5; 2-7; 9-12 | years; years; months | CPC-ENSOFAQ | 2026-10-23 |
| cpc:oni-def | ONI: 3-month running mean of ERSST.v6 SST anomalies in the Nino 3.4 region (5N-5S, 120-170W), based on centred 30-year base periods updated every 5 years; warm and cold periods where the threshold +/-0.5 degC is met for at least 5 consecutive overlapping seasons; recent values may change up to two months after posting; table continues but RONI is official (PNS 26-05) | 0.5; 5 | degC; seasons | CPC-ONI-V6 | 2026-10-23 |
| pns:roni | from 1 February 2026 CPC uses the Relative Oceanic Nino Index: El Nino (La Nina) when the relative SST anomaly is +0.5 (-0.5) degC or more over 3 consecutive months in Nino 3.4; relative anomaly = Nino 3.4 anomaly minus the mean SST anomaly of the global tropics (20N-20S), rescaled to the amplitude of the traditional index; historical events: five or more consecutive overlapping 3-month seasons; less sensitive to the base period, better related to tropical Pacific rainfall; legacy ONI files still updated | 0.5 | degC | NWS-PNS-26-05 | 2026-10-23 |
| cpc:edd-2026-10 | 8 October 2026: El Nino Advisory; El Nino strengthened, SST anomalies above +4.0 degC in parts of the eastern equatorial Pacific; Nino-3.4 +2.1, Nino-3 +3.0, Nino-1+2 +3.9 degC last month; subsurface anomalies above +10 degC at depth in the east; deeper-than-average thermocline; low-level westerly anomalies; enhanced convection central to eastern Pacific, suppressed over Indonesia; SOI significantly negative; chance of exceeding +2.5 degC RONI (beyond all events since 1950): 54 % SON, 83 % OND, 70 % NDJ; strong-to-very strong El Nino likely through JFM 2027 (> 83 %) | 2.1; 4.0; 2.5 | degC | CPC-EDD-2026-10 | 2026-10-23 |
| cpc:nao-teledoc | NAO: one of the most prominent teleconnection patterns in all seasons; north-south dipole of anomalies, one centre over Greenland and the other of opposite sign over the central North Atlantic at 35-40N; positive phase: below-normal pressure at high latitudes, above-normal over the central North Atlantic, eastern US and western Europe; changes in the North Atlantic jet stream and storm track; positive phase: above-average temperatures in the eastern US and northern Europe, below over Greenland and often southern Europe; wetter northern Europe and Scandinavia in winter, drier southern and central Europe; negative phase dominated mid-1950s to 1978/79, positive from 1979/80 to 1994/95; monthly index since 1950, standardized | 35-40 | deg N | CPC-NAO-TELEDOC | 2026-10-23 |
| climategov:nao | NAO index: strength of the Icelandic (sub-polar) Low and the Azores (subtropical) High; positive when both stronger than average, larger pressure difference, stronger Atlantic jet and northward-shifted storm track: northern Europe stormier, wetter and warmer, southern Europe drier, eastern North America fewer cold-air outbreaks; negative phase the opposite; late 18th century a missionary noted mild Greenland winters with severe Danish winters and vice versa; NAO the most prominent pattern of climate variability in the mid and high northern latitudes; strongly correlated with the Arctic Oscillation | - | - | CLIMATEGOV-NAO | 2026-10-23 |
| climategov:enso-history | ENSO is a coupled climate phenomenon with three phases (El Nino, La Nina, neutral); during El Nino the easterly surface winds along the equator weaken or reverse, rainfall drops over Indonesia and rises over the tropical Pacific; South American fishermen noticed coastal warming around Christmas and called it El Nino (the boy child); Sir Gilbert Walker discovered the Southern Oscillation without linking it to the ocean; in the late 1960s Jacob Bjerknes and others connected ocean and atmosphere: ENSO; La Nina and neutral gained prominence in the 1980s | - | - | CLIMATEGOV-ENSO-NUTSHELL | 2026-10-23 |
| ipccgl:modes | modes of climate variability: recurrent space-time structures of natural variability with intrinsic spatial patterns, seasonality and time scales, arising from atmospheric dynamics or ocean-atmosphere coupling; many driven by internal processes, a source of predictability on sub-seasonal to decadal time scales; climate variability: deviations from a mean state at all scales beyond individual weather events, internal or forced | - | - | IPCC-AR6-GLOSSARY | 2026-10-23 |
| ipccgl:index | climate index: a time series constructed from climate variables that summarizes the state of the climate system, e.g. the Iceland minus Azores sea-level pressure difference for the NAO; often principal components; anomaly: deviation from the value averaged over a reference period; reference period: period over which statistics are calculated | - | - | IPCC-AR6-GLOSSARY | 2026-10-23 |
| ipccgl:teleconnection | teleconnection: association between climate variables at widely separated, fixed locations related through physical processes and oceanic or atmospheric pathways; caused by Rossby wave-trains, jet and storm-track displacements, AMOC and Walker circulation fluctuations; can be initiated by modes, with lags | - | - | IPCC-AR6-GLOSSARY | 2026-10-23 |
| ipccgl:enso | ENSO: El Nino first a warm current along Ecuador and Peru disrupting the fishery, now warming of the tropical Pacific east of the date line; associated with the Southern Oscillation, a global-scale tropical and subtropical surface pressure pattern; coupled, preferred time scales two to about seven years; measured by the Tahiti-Darwin pressure difference or central and eastern equatorial Pacific SST; global teleconnections | 2-7 | years | IPCC-AR6-GLOSSARY | 2026-10-23 |
| ipccgl:monsoon | global monsoon: global-scale solstitial mode dominating the annual variation of tropical and subtropical precipitation; domain where local summer minus winter mean precipitation exceeds 2.5 mm/day; South and South East Asian monsoon: pronounced seasonal reversals of wind and precipitation, starts late May/early June, progresses north-east, ends late September/early October; West African monsoon: seasonal reversal, moist south-westerlies from the Gulf of Guinea progress north May-September, rainfall maximum near 12N in late August/September, retreat from October | 2.5 | mm/day | IPCC-AR6-GLOSSARY | 2026-10-23 |
| ipccgl:other-modes | NAO: leading mode of North Atlantic atmospheric variability, see-saw of sea-level pressure between the Azores High and the Icelandic Low, fluctuations of the westerlies and of the cyclones and fronts; IOD: east-west dipole of tropical Indian Ocean SST anomalies, positive phase cooling off Sumatra and Java and warming off Somalia, develops in boreal summer, matures in autumn, affects rainfall in Australia, South East Asia, East Africa; SAM: leading mode of Southern Hemisphere pressure, strength and latitude of the westerly belt; PDV: decadal Pacific variability beyond ENSO, includes the PDO | - | - | IPCC-AR6-GLOSSARY | 2026-10-23 |
| nws4:monsoon | monsoon: a thermally driven wind arising from differential heating between a land mass and the adjacent ocean that reverses its direction seasonally | - | - | NWS-GLOSSARY-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| imd:lpa | monsoon season (June-September) rainfall over India as a whole: long period average 88 cm, standard deviation 9 cm (about 10 %); normal when within 90-110 % of LPA; current LPA 880.6 mm (1961-2010); JJAS 2018 804.1 mm; June 2018 155.7 mm; Kerala LPA June 556, July 659, August 427, September 252 mm | 88; 9; 880.6; 804.1 | cm; cm; mm; mm | IMD-MONSOON-FAQ | 2026-10-23 |
| imd:heat-low | heat low: as the Sun moves north the lands around the Arabian Sea receive large heat input, surface heat flux about 160 W/m2 in June over arid north-west India, Pakistan and the Middle East; a trough of low pressure forms, very shallow (to 850 hPa, 1.5 km) with a ridge above; little rain; intense heat low draws moist air along the monsoon trough; weak heat low in 1987, a drought year; monsoon trough: elongated low from the heat low over Pakistan to the head of the Bay of Bengal, its oscillations give active and break phases | 160; 1.5 | W/m2; km | IMD-MONSOON-FAQ | 2026-10-23 |
| imd:onset | IMD declares monsoon onset over Kerala (criteria adopted 2016) from daily rainfall at 14 stations over Kerala and nearby, the wind field and outgoing longwave radiation over the south-east Arabian Sea; withdrawal from north-west India declared only after 1 September, after 5 days without rain, a lower-tropospheric anticyclone and drier air | 14; 5 | stations; days | IMD-MONSOON-FAQ | 2026-10-23 |
| narang24:enso-monsoon | ENSO and the IOD are the major sources of predictability of Indian summer monsoon rainfall; El Nino events tend to reduce rainfall over India (deficits in 1904, 1972, 1987) and vice versa (1910, 1933, 1988); in 1997 a positive IOD coincided with a strong El Nino yet India had near-normal rainfall; positive IOD defined with the Dipole Mode Index | - | - | NARANG-2024 | 2026-10-23 |
| thirumalai24:bjerknes | record-breaking El Nino events of 1982, 1997 and 2015 (>= 2 K in the central Pacific) drove coral bleaching and mortality, tropical forest fires, heat waves; the growth of El Nino events is driven by the Bjerknes feedback, the positive feedback loop between SSTs, winds and ocean currents in the equatorial Pacific; El Nino initiated when random fluctuations in the trade winds weaken the equatorial currents that keep warm water in the west; warm pool water expands east, convection shifts to the central Pacific, the trades weaken further; La Nina events are triggered by a preceding El Nino | 2 | K | THIRUMALAI-2024 | 2026-10-23 |

| nasa:milank-detail | NH summers currently about 4.5 days longer than winters; perihelion on or about January 3, aphelion about July 4, distance difference about 5.1 million km (3.4 %); eccentricity slowly decreasing; global annual insolation change from eccentricity very small; obliquity between 22.1 and 24.5 deg over the last million years, now 23.4 and decreasing, last maximum about 10,000 years ago, minimum about 10,000 years from now; larger tilt favours deglaciation, high latitudes change most; axial precession about 25,771.5 years, apsidal precession about 112,000 years, combined about 23,000 years on average; perihelion now in NH winter, flips in about 13,000 years; Milankovitch thought obliquity most important; ice ages at 41,000-year intervals between one and three million years ago, lengthened to 100,000 years about 800,000 years ago, no clear explanation yet; Milankovitch died 1958, notice about 10 years later; Hays et al. 1976, 450,000 years | 4.5; 5.1; 22.1-24.5; 25771.5; 112000; 23000; 41000; 800000 | days; million km; deg; yr; yr; yr; yr; yr | NASA-MILANKOVITCH-B4 | 2026-10-23 |
| eo00:milankovitch | Milankovitch born 1879 in Dalj; built a mathematical model of the latitudinal differences of insolation and corresponding surface temperature for 600,000 years before 1800; at the suggestion of the climatologist Koppen chose summer insolation at 65 N, reasoning that great ice sheets grew near this latitude and that cooler summers reduce snowmelt, giving a positive snow budget; theory largely ignored for about 50 years until Hays et al. 1976; eccentricity cycle 90,000-100,000 years; obliquity 22.1-24.5 deg, about 40,000 years; snow-covered Earth reflects more sunlight, a positive feedback | 1879; 600000; 65 | -; yr; deg N | EO-MILANKOVITCH-2000 | 2026-10-23 |
| barker21:terminations | terminations describe the major transitions marking the end of glacial cycles (Late Pleistocene); dominant period of climate variability over the last half million years close to 100 kyr although eccentricity forcing at that period is negligible compared with obliquity and precession; benthic d18O asymmetric: stepwise build-up over about 90 kyr, then about 10 kyr of fast decay (saw-tooth); terminations must involve non-linearities to amplify modest insolation forcing; Antarctic CO2 rises on a millennial time scale whenever Atlantic circulation is weakened; Denton et al.: meltwater suppresses Atlantic circulation, CO2 released, beyond a threshold interglacial conditions stabilise | 100; 90; 10 | kyr | BARKER-KNORR-2021 | 2026-10-23 |
| kohler20:mpt | changes in Earth's orbit are the fundamental reason for the Quaternary ice ages, but orbital theory alone cannot explain the evolution from 41-kyr glacial cycles to an average 100-kyr periodicity during the last 1 Myr (the Mid-Pleistocene Transition); candidate feedbacks: land ice dynamics, possibly driven by regolith removal, and the gradual decline of atmospheric CO2 during glacial minima; a glacial termination is realised in 67, 88 and 52 % of the obliquity cycles of the early, middle and late Quaternary (interglacials defined by little northern land ice outside Greenland) | 67; 88; 52 | % | KOHLER-VANDEWAL-2020 | 2026-10-23 |

| ouVM:textures | granoblastic: polygonal grain boundaries meeting at about 120 degrees (triple junctions); porphyroblastic: very large crystals, millimetres to several centimetres, typically garnet, staurolite, cordierite, chloritoid; poikiloblastic: very large crystals (poikiloblasts) containing a plethora of inclusions, typically staurolite, garnet and cordierite; ophitic: subcategory of poikilitic texture in which euhedral plagioclase laths are enclosed by pyroxene, in dolerite, gabbro and basalt; corona: new minerals surrounding a mineral breaking down from chemical disequilibrium, in igneous and metamorphic rocks | 120 | deg | OU-VM-TEXTURES | 2026-10-23 |
